FROM A BED in which she has lain crippled 
for more than thirty years, a smiling woman not 
only manages her own house but succeeds in help- 
ing other people. 


When I went to see her (writes a “Daily Mirror” 
ccrrespondent) she drew aside the lace curtains, 
popped her head through the window of her little 
villa, smiled a welcome, and handed me a latch key. 


“Come inside If you would like | Blakes Life Worth While 
to talk to me,” she said, 

Florence has made life worth 
while by making people depend 
on her, Her sister relies on her 
for cooking and compantonship. 

Nobody who has been on 

A.R.P. duty during the night 

callg in valn for a refreshing 

cup of tea. 

Florence has her room com- 
pletely organised. In a corner- 
near the bed is an 8ft. long poker 
for stirring the fire. A table’ 
is laden with all the articles she 
needs for her own and other pzo- 
ple’s meals. Within reach is the 
radio, and on the electric stove a 
I kettle is hardly ever off the boil. 


This is the 
Richards, 
chester, 
tors, 


way Miss Florence 
of Burnage-lane, Man- 
always greets her visi- 


Through the window = she 
takes in all her rations, attends 
to the rent and the insurance 
agent and chats with the nelgh- 
bours. 


From her bed, Florence, not 
half as old as her fifty-four years, 
Jooks out with cheerfulness and 
contidence on a world which has 
denied her .so much.

CSREAKERS? 


Is there such a thing as 
mental telepathy? Scien- 
tists have pooh-poohed 
the idea for years, but an 
eminent authority states 
that he believes from his 
study of twins that some- 
thing very close to mental 
telepathy is existing in 
the world. 


Twin sisters were in the same 
biology class. 


In an examination the main 
question dealt with material that 
‘one girl claims to have neither 
ses nor discussed with her sister. 


When she began to think about 
the matter, however, the answer 
came to her and she wrote it out 
without difficulty. 

This answer was 60 much like 
that written by her twin sister 
that the teacher suspected copy- 
Ing, but the girls had been sit- 
ting far apart. 


Another instance tells of a boy 
called Joe and his twin. These 
two boys answered q iestions on 
examination papers identically. 

One teacher, despairing of pre- 
venting this; Kind of communtca- 
tion, put Joe in the office of the 
principul and kept Joe’s twin in 
the teacher’s own office. Joe was 
given the questions, but did not 
begin to answer them. 

“Why don’t you get to work?’ 
inquired the teacher. 

“IT am not ready,” 
Joe. ‘i 

He did not start to answer the 
examination questions until the 
actual mOment when his twin 
brother had recelved his ques- 
tlons and then he went busily to 
work. And the papers they 
turned in were exactly the same 

—even down to the mistakes. 


responded

He was bleading heavily. 

But’ he made his way down| 
a ladder, then’ down five flights, 
of efeven stairs each and swit- 
ched off the electric current to 
stop the machinery 
This story of amazing fortitude 

was told at the inquest at Lewis-

of Bourne-way. Hayes, Kent. 

He worked at Woolwich 
Arsenal, where the accident 
occurred.

he collapsed’ and was taken to 
hospital, where he survived for 
seventeen days.

‘Beethoven did not know 


the Morse code. But he 
wrote ‘in it. By the sheer- 


est and yet most appro-| 


priate coincidence pos- 
sible, he employed as a 
rhythmic pattern in the 
first movement of his 
Fifth Symphony what any 
telegrapher would recog- 
nise as the Morse symbol 
for the letter V. 


There are two coin- 
cidences here, one of them 
obvious and of no im- 
portance. The letter V is 
the Roman equivalent of 
the figure 5, but what 
matters more is that V 

.has become in France 
and other occupied coun- 
tries a symbol of ultimate; 
victory over the Nazi ter- 
ror. It is written on walls 
and elsewhere to catch 
the eye of Nazi overseers. 

It is ubiquitous. So many 
V's have appeared as to 

suggest some sort of dn- 

derground organisation. 

As long ago as April this 
“newspaper reported on 
this movement. 

‘Now, like an increasing- 
ly unruly child, ‘V is not 
only going to be seen but 
heard. For victims of 
Nazi ambition are now 
learning to spell it out in 
dots and dashes; dot-det- 
dot-dash. They ‘can even 
sing or whistle it, to the 
first four notes of the 
world’s most familiar 
symphony. If Frenchmen 
do this as often as senti- 
ment prompts they will 
reproduce most of the 
symphony’s first move- 
ment, which is based on a 
constant reiteration of 
dot-dot-dot-dash motifs, 
one set overlaying another 
in crescendos sometimes 
expressive of an increas- 
ing fury, as of suppressed 
peoples. 

“Nazis seem apprehen- 
sive over this secret -wea- 
pon. They.are trying to 
cause confusion by adopt- 
ing:Vas:a‘symibol of ‘Nazi 
victory. But those who 
write or sound V for free- 
dom can, as “Colonel V. 
' Britton” has-suggested in 

‘ a broadcast naming ‘July 
20 as ‘'V' Day,” -teave the 
” confusion “where it be- 
ae jiongs—in Berlin.” 

» <"Pheé ‘Nazis may “find,

~~" "(By JAY ALLEN)” 


(An American war correspo 


md@ent here tells the story of his 


arrest and tmprisonment for four months by the German forces in 
eccupied: France and discloses information he gleaned from other 


prisoners and Nazt guards). 


THE “LINE OF DEMARCATION” between oc~ 
cupied France and the part called free is one of thé 
most interesting features of the Franco-German 
armistice agreement of June, 1940, 

Originally, it was only what its name indicates. 


But it has been developed 
ing Vichy. 

In March,, when this corres- 
pondent crossed it en route to 
Paris and got caught on his way 
out, the squeeze was on. Admiral 
Francois Darlan, Vichy’s Vice 
Premier, had not yet won the full 
confidence of the German Gov- 
ernment, which was still sulking 
over the dismissal of Pierre Laval 
on Dec. 13, The line was to all 
intent closed to the passage of 
persons, goods and money. Ger- 
man customs guards had been 
brought in to replace soldiery, 
who were both careless and cor- 
ruptible. They brought police 
dogs and began = setting land 
mines at night. 

The vary oxie’ence of the de- 
tmarcation line is a challenge to 
the French, who are an ingenl- 
ous race. Most everybody has 
his favourite combination for 
smuggling tetters. Thousands 
of ptreons manage to siip 
through every day. The French 
have been used to moving freely 
in thelr own country ara the 
habl. is hard to break. | 


A Few Pay With 
Their Lives 


Perhaps one in 10,000 pays with 
his life. A friend of mine, an 
army officer, father of six chil- 
dren, was brought down on the 
line near Chalon- while I was in 
jail there. Perhaps one fn 5,090 
gets a wound; we had several lads 
in the infirmary with legs or arms 
shattered by explosive bullets. 

For every person who is caught 
on the line fifty to a hundred 
get through. The penalty when 
you are caught is two to three 
weeks in jail or a fine of from 
500 .francs to whatever you hap- 
pen to have on you. For letters 
you can get anything from one to 
twenty months. 

Regardiess of whether the 
squceze is on or off, American 
oorrespondents in Vichy cannot 
get passes for the occupied 
zone, not even those among 
them who have proved their 
sympathy for Laval. Darian and 
Franco-German ‘collaboration,”" 
So Kenneth Downs of Inter- 

national News and I arranged for 
a quiet trip to Paris. In Vichy 
where there are something like 
250 agents of the ‘Gestapo, you 
don't mention . such. plans. In 


Lyon we picked up Paul, Thiriet. ¢ 


One-time assistant to Pertinax on 
the Echo de Paris,. tvho, .because 
of this connection‘ and his candid 
expressions of épMion, had been 
forbidden to practice his profes- 
sion in Vichy. ..He has since man- 
aged to leave France, 

From Lyon we took one of the 
several taxi services .to. the line: 
The Germans seem to know about 
these and ocasionally’ send. a 
Gestapo man witha party-to see 
who fs along. Sometimes arrests 
are made and sometimes not; We 
drove; to..St... Eusebe,..a hamlet in 


into a device for squeez- 


gate and were onto the highway 
— In occupied France. Our job 
now was to set back our watghes 
an hour, since occupied France 
is on Central European time and 
and the customs guards always 
ask the the time of their suspects. 
Our job was to reach the road 
into Montchanin before the patrol 
came back. 

We passed German 
who must have guessed that 
we had just crossed, for we 
were smeared with mud, but 
none seemed Interested In us. 
From Montchanin we took a 

branch line to Chagny. There the 
Lyon-Paris express arrived with 
only the crew on board; all of the 
passengers had been taken off rt 
Chalon-sur-Saone for “verifica- 
tion” of their passes. 

By the time we reached Paris, 
German soldiers against the 
French landscape had ceased t6 
be a novelty. Since we confined 
our conversation to French, of a 
sort, they pajd no attention to us. 
We arrived in Paris at 11 at night. 

Mr. Downs and I had come in 
together because we thought that 
we would be a comfort to each 
other if failed. But he had orders 
to get to.Syria at the earliest pus- 
sible moment. He and M. Thiriet 
got back safely across the line ut 
‘Montchanin. .I missed my clipper 
thereafter. - 

I went everywhere, or almost, 
in the nine days I stayed in Paris. 
I avoided. friends and particularly 
the American correspondents, 
thinking that they might be 
watched. It would seem that 
their movements are pretty much 
circumscribed. 

Later on, in the months I spent 
in Chalon_ prison, only one, 
George Axtlisson, of “The New 
York Time,” an old friend, was 
able to get down to see me.

From Wionceau To

(By / A Cavhenirondets ‘aboard A Catalina 4 Flying: 
Boat, On Atlantic Patrol) 


“AIRCRAFT AHEAD! s 


The pilot’s curt an- 


nouncement and the shriek of. the alarm horn. trans- 


formed the laughing group of _ twenty-year-olds. |; 


aboard the American-made Royal Air Force patrol . 
‘plane into fighting men with itching. trigger fingers. 

The youth from London stopped slicing bread 
in his capacity as utility cook and crawled into the’ 
tail to man the rear machine gun. The port and 
starboard gunners in their “blisters” on the sides of 


the craft unlimbered their guns. 


The observer 


rushed to man the forward gun. 


For the rst time this corres- 
pondent felt. that a grandstand 
seat in the Battle of the Atlantic 
was not exactly comfortable. 


Through binoculars the plane 
two miles uhead looked like any 
other ‘plane, but the pilot said it 
was a two-motored Dornier 115. 

The Catalina kept on its 
course, but the danger passed. 

Apparently the 'plane ahead 

was on reconatssance and didn’t 

want to fight. The crew mem- 
bers were disappointed. 

The crews love their “cats,” 
praise their maneuverability and 
damn their long range. Catalinas 
can fly so far and so long that 


in red. In it my friend gave the 
location of a dozen or so cemen! 
fuctorigs in the north of France 
working, he said, on orders for 
defensive coastal fortifications, on 
which he gave precise details. Both 
factories and fortifications de- 
scribed in relation to landmarks 
that could be identified from the 
air. 

On the last page, underilned 

In green, was a suggestion that 

“you know who” should be 

tipped off to bomb the cement 

plants, Very careless, my friend. 

} wae to hlame for not having 

read this report. 

I placed my hand open on the 
table and then withdrew it, drag- 
ging the two paces with it Tlet 
them fall on my lap and then 
quietly, lest they crackle. folded 
them and slipped them under my: 
belt. 

I made a brief formal state- 
ment and signed it. The captain 
suid that the court-martial in Le 
Creusot would pass on my ciase— 
in a couple of days. 


The Court-Riartial At 
Le Creusot 


In Le Creusot I was. parked. 
for a time with the German 


Hig, equivalent of our military police. 


They introduced me _ to their 
police dog wha I- was. told 
path jn an average, of two 
s aday. I got.a huge 
platter of roast porly and 
potatoes, a Gernian solditr’s -ra- 
The French people often go 
meat .at ae 

After lunch I was taken to. 
requisitioned villa on a hiliside 
overlooking: Schneider’s vast 
arms plant,, There was a ‘sign 
over the gate “Kriegsgericht” | 
(court-martial). 

There,. for seven hours, I .was

have to.sustain an-elaborate He. - 


patrols average eighteen to 
twenty hours, The Catalina 
which spotted the German battle- 
ship Bismark was in the air 
twenty-seven hours. 


Particular Job 


Our particular job was to look 
for submarines and other signs 
of the enemy. But Catalinas— 
many of whose crews fly a dis- 
tance each week comparable to 
four crossings of the se ropa —_ 
are used also for 
“strikes” and as convoy siete 


When sent on “strike” the 
twin-eng'ned ‘planes are out to 
sink submarines, not merely 
report them. They fly where 
submarines have been report- 
ed and go after them with anti- 
submarine devices and ox- 
plosives. 

On escort duty, Catalinas sup- 
ply almost. twenty-four-hour air 
protection for convoys in the dan- 
gerous sea approaches to Great 
Britain. On patrol duty Catalinas 
reduce the effectiveness of sub- 
marines. German commandery 
keep their craft submerged when 
‘planes appear. 


New Time Record 


The crew members sald that 
sinee the Catalinas have been 
operating over the Atiantic sub- 
marines have established a new 
time record for crash dives ©: 
thirty-two seconds. Some U-boats 
have dived with men on deck, 
they told, sacriNcing them in thetr 
haste. How many submarines have 
been sunk by Catalinas cannot 
be reported, but it may be said 
that the number fg, constantly in- 
creasing. 

Patrol crews have time oc- 
casionally to think up wild 
schemes. The pilot told one. 

“The idea is," the sald, “to 

{hrow green paint Into the 

ocean where a submarine Is 

ceported. Later, when the sub- 
marine come to the surface, the 

Palnt will cover the periscape 
“and the commander wilt think’ 

whe is stilt’ under water. and 

keep on rising. 

“When the submarine . reaches 


‘potled! an altitude of about 2,000 feet, we|- 


will come along and shoot ‘pin. 
down.”

“The ‘fitst day's fighting berice 
Sollum ended in our men. disput- 
ing possession of: ‘the Halfnaya 
(Hellfire Pags); At dawn next day, 
under cover of a heavy ground 
mist, our attack was renewed. 
Both the weather and the terrain 
suited the Highlandets, who form- 

the left flank. Within two 
hours. the enemy ‘was driven out 
of fortified positions at the head 
of the pass. 


“Indian troops on the right 
encountered stronger opposition, 
and.never did the pass so well de- 
serve its lurid nickname as it did 
tnat day, when it echoed inces- 
santly to.the murderous challenge 
of machine-gun fire. 


“Meanwhile, with the High- 
Janders. holding the head of the 
pass, another column of our in- 
tantry, supported by a squadron 
of tanks, advanced along the 
ridge of the escarpment, where it 
sweeps in a vast semi-circle to- 
ares the headland behind Sol- 
um. 


“On this headland stands an old 
Egyptian barracks, gleaming 
white against the brown sand. The 
German garrison there, about a 
battalion strong, abandoned their 
position, and, withthe town be- 
low: no longer tenable, made their 
way along the shores of Sollum 
Bay to the: mouth of Hellfire Pass. 
But there was n0. escape by the 
pass. The only hope was to with- 
draw to the beach’ and make a 
stand with their backs to the 
sea. 


Tables Turned 


“There was a -cértain grim, if 
short-lived, satisfaction in the 
knowledge. that for -once was 
the enemy called on to defend 
himsetf# on a beach. 


the Indians in 
Hellfire Pass, fighting tirelessly, 
made good progress, and a few 
Germans who had escaped death 
or capture manned isolated 
machine-guns on the western 
slopes of the pass. From these 
posts they kept up a harassing fire, 
which took a fairly heavy toll of 
the Indians.

throughout the night. From time

. “The next enemy counter-at- 
tack developed far to the south 
beyond Sidi Omar, 


“A formidable Panzer (arm- 
qured). force sighted from the 
air was too good a chance for the 
RA.F. to miss. Nine Blenheims, 
flying in tight formation from a 
height of 3,000 feet, scattered tanks 
ae armoured forces in all direc- 
tions. 


Ten tanks were among the 
wreckage strewn on the desert, 
but the threat of an encircling 
movement was too serious to be 
Ignored, and the order was 
given for a general withdrawal 
to our orlginal positions. - 


“As our troops pulled back from 
the Hellfire Pass along the ‘Via 
Mussolini’, Panzer squadrons 
Joomed over the coastal escarp- 
ment, They remained poised there 
for a moment, and then poured 
down the slopes like dark torrents 
and raced across the coastal plain 
—just too late.”

BOYS _ 
CAPTURE 
NAZI 


Three evacuee boys cap- 
tured a wounded Nazi air- 
man, and took him to a 
police station three miles 
away. 


The boys, 17-year-old twins, 
Edward and David Smith and 
Desmond Mercer; aged 15, came 
across the aitman after they had 
been to see the wreckage of. his 
‘plane, one of: the bombers shot 
down by our fighters at the night. 


“We had driven about two miles 
on our homeward journey,” they 
said, “when we saw the airman 
lying beside a lonely road, waving 
his arm feebly. 

“He asked us for water, but 
someone <else:came along and 
gave him: mil’ and cucumber

THE CHINA MAIL, SEPTEMBER 2, 1941... 


| VICHY. 


American and French 
conceptions of the world 
were pictured as’ com- 
pletely opposed in a state- 
ment given to the Ameri- 
can press in Paris by 
Fernand de Brinon, re- 
presentative of the Vichy 
government in occupied 
France. 


“One conception of the world [s 
that of Great Britain, to which 
Roosevelt has subscribed.” sald 
M. de Brinon, “Another is based 
upon socialism of a national type, 
which means a new distribution of 
the wealth of the globe, The 
French Government considers that 
it is in the interests of France to 
espouse the latter conception and 
to work for its accomplishment.” 


M. de Brinon's statement was 
inspired by that of Under-Secre- 
tary of Strate Sumner Welles to a 
press conference concerning 
French policy over the defence of 
Indo-China, 
derstanding with France arose 
from a fundamental difference of 
views, and he pretested what he 
alleged owas the effort of the 
United States Government to bring 
pressure upon Fratice and to in- 
fluence her policy. 


“To understand very well’ he 
said, “that each nation must be 
guided by its own interests. Emi- 
nent American personalities have 
come out openly against the 
National Revolution. Roosevelt has 
banished Fascism, National Social- 
ism, the Spanish Falange and all 
other movements which have re- 
jected the Marxist democracy. 


“At the same time, in America 
they are taking unto themselves 
the right to forbid the French Gov- 
ernment to collaborate politically 
with Germany, and are threaten- 
ing France in the event of her dis- 
regard of these counsels, Such 
an attitude is unacceptable to the 
French peopite, which has the right 
to chouse for itself its methods of 
recovery. 


“Petain defined those methods 
when he decided to ask for en 
armistice and when he met the 
Fuehrer at Montoire. If Petain is 
of the opinion that parliamentary 
democracy has done a great deal 
of harm to France and should be 
considered (a thing of the © past, 
and if he has decided to seek every 
means of coming to an honourable 
understanding with the victor, that 
is his own business and not thal of 
Mr, Roosevelt and Mr. Welles.”

series, the blue-jackets recovered from their

Hong Kong Baseballers 
v. South China the 
League finale, resulted 
in Mohawks over-running 
Caroliners 18 to 6 before 
a handful of spectators, 
whilst the Bees.shaded the 
Aces 4 to 2 tn an exhibi- 
tion tilt staged by the 
pupils of Grandpa Leung's 
Baseball School. 


All Lau, who started on the 
hill for the Chinese, was derrick- 
ed in favour of Wally Ching after 
he had conceded three safeties. 
For the rest of the game Ching: 
fanned three und walked one, 
and was robbed of a no-hit no- 
run performance by allowing Lou 
Pawloski to nick him for a 
double. Tony Mascavage, work- 
ing on the slab for the _ losers, 
yielded seven blows, strick-out 
one and walked three. 


Short-lived Lead 


Three Chinese batters paraded 
to the plate and back 
again in the first inning for a 
scoveless frame, whilst the Ameri- 
cans tallied three times on three 
blows to jump into a short-lived 
lead. In the second frame Morocco 
Chan singled and pilfered second. 
-whilst Hank Chan was given a 
life on third-sacker Bowersox’'s 
fumble. Al Lau trotted down the 
tine to load the sacks, after work- 
ing moundsman Mascav‘'age for a 
walk on four pitched balls. 

Coming up into the batter's 
box with runners dancing on the 
base-trails, Pete Choy socked tho 
apple on the nose for a two-run 
double. Dick Chung’s “Japanese 
liner’ to right chased two more 
mates over the plate for the 
Chinese to draw ahead, and be- 
fore the dieastrous inning was 
over Hank Ali dumped a neat 
bunt along first to ecore Ghung. 

In the next semester the Chin- 
ese started off by loading the sacks 
on a bingle, a miscue and a walk 
to produce another cluster of five 
counters, after they had batted 
right around. In the fourth and 
the fifth they were horse-collared, 
but in the sixth the Chinese 
stowed the game away with an- 
other marker on a walk and a 
sacrifice,

After being blanked for five 
chapters, the Americans staged a 
belated uprising in the last stanza, 

. but the threat was smothered -as 
. Earl Wilson popped out to first- 
cr sicker Hank Chan.

All-Stars Score 


Inaugurating the Initial canto, 
Nip Lum stashed out a single on

By Brawback: 


The one. great drawback. for the 
Army is the lack of facilities for nets 
practice—at least insofar as ‘other 
ranks” are concerned—and,. in this con- 
nection it woud perbaps.be- worth the 
while of the Sports Board to consider 
returfing part of the ground at Sookun- 
poo for this purpose. 

Owing to the fact that the different 
unfts arc scattered all aver the Colony, 
organised-:practice. {s almost an Impos- 
sibility for the-Army, and knock-ups .on 
cement pitches, in barrack compounds, 
have served for practices in the past~— 
and will probably continue to do so for 
many years to come, 

In spite of all this, however, Army 
have always been able to provide the 
Colony with a number of really good 
all-round crickelers. and {tt would ap- 
pear that 1941-42 will be no exception. 

Now that the Army have openiy 
stated: they: are, ready. for.. the, season, 
it 4s to be hoped ‘that the Cricket 
League will rise from its prolonged 
Summer slumber and set the wheels 
in motion for another enjoyable season.

OCCASION OF LABOUR DAY. 


The President continued: 


“American 


labour now bears. a tremendous responsibility 
in winning this most brutal and most terrible 


of all wars. 
“In our factories, 


shops and arsenals we 


are building weapons on a great scale, great 


in its magnitude. To 


all the battle-fronts of 


the world are these weapons being despatch- 
ed by day and night over seas and through the 
air, and this nation is now devising and deve- 
loping new weapons of unprecedented power 
toward the maintenance of Democracy. 


“Why are we doing this? Why 


ure we determined to devote our, 


entire industrial effort to the 
prosecution of a war which ac- 
tually has not yet touched our 
own shores? 

“We are not a warlike people; 
we never sought glory as a 
nation of warriors. We are not 
interested in aggression. We are 
not interested -- as the Dictators 
are —- in looting. We do not 
covet one square inch of the 
territory of any other nation 

“Our vast effort and the unity 
of purpose which inspires that 
effort are due solely to our re- 
cognition of the fuct that our 
fundamental rights — including 
the rights of Labour -— are 
threatened by Hitler's violent at- 
tempt to rule the world. 


The Bond 


There never has been a moment 
in history when the Americans 
were not ready to stand up as 
free men and fight for — their 
rights. In times of nationat 
emergency one fact is brought 
home clearly to us décisively -- 
that all our rights are inter- 
dependent. 

The rights of freedom 
worship would mean nothing 
without. freedom. of speech and. 
the rights of free fabour, as we 
krtow them to-day, could not 
survive without rights of free 
enterprise. That is an in-; 
destructable bond between ~all. 
ua Americans. 

Inter-denéndencé of interests, 
‘privileges and. opportunities — 
‘ inter-dependgnce . 0. rights. that 
is what unites us—men and wo- 
‘men of. all. sections. That is why |. 
we have. been ablé lefy. and. 
frustrate enemies who believed 
ithey could divide us and control 
jus from within,

(By OTEO D. TOLISCHUS) 


_ PRERARATIONS. FOR the American-British- 
Soviet military conference in Moscow have caused 


ment of war materials, because such an under- 
standing will have the greatest influence not only 
on the Axis States grouped around Germany and 
Italy in Europe but also upon Soviet-Japanese rela- 


tions. 


“The Soviet Union's participa- 
tion in the joint front with Brit- 
ain und the Uniled States, as; 
manifested — by the proposed: 
Moscow conference, indicates the 
Soviet has fallen into the crafty 
manoeuvres of Britain. 

If anything should happen to 
impair Soviet-Japanese rela- 
tions, which have gr¢gatly im- 
proved since the conclusion of 
the neutrality pact, the resgon- 
sibility will rest with the Krem- 
lin.” 


See Full Encirclement 


According td Captain Hideo 
Hiraide, navy spokesman, Japaa 
und the United States are already 
at war economically; with the 
United States trying to cut off 
Japan’s access to strategic raw 
matzrials. Furthermore, he says, 
the United States and Britain 
have completed their encircle- 
ment of the front side of Japan by 


naval and air bases running from 
the Aleutian Islands in the north 
to Australia, with Singapore as 
the central base for military 
operations by Britain, the United 
States, Netherlands Indies, Aus- 
tralia and China, 

Captain Hiraide suspects the 
United States is seeking bases 
in Siberia and China in return 
fof aid to the Soviet and Chung- 
king, completing the encircle- 


ment from the rear. 


Jing of Japanese

| téerview with °- 
“| Van ‘Noorhii 


“A step further,” Captain 
Hiraide warns, ‘and Japan will be 
driven to make a decision 
involving her life or death No 
nation with any legitimate claim | 
to life can be expected to accept 
its doom without question or @ 
struggle.” 

He further declares the United 
States underestimates Japan's 
strength and adds: 

“If foreign people had a cor- 
rect idea of Japan's real power ! 
they would never think of chal- 
Jenging her.” 

Commander Tasniro of the: 
Navy ‘Ministry’s publicity depart- 
ment declared ‘before a gather- 
newspaper and 
magazine writers: 

“Our imperial navy is fully re- 


{solved to undergo all privations 


for the sake of the nation, come 
what. may our imperial mavy has 
passed the stage of dependence ‘on 
foréign assistance and ‘has 
advanced to the position. where it 
must win all necessary domina- 
tion of the sea.” !

DYING, 
HE DID 
HIS JOB 


A man slipped on a 
piece of coal while at work 
and fell against some re- 
volving machinery. 

The machinery tore off his 
jeft arm and shoulder blade. 


He was bleeding heavily. 
But he made his way down 


of eleven stairs cach and 8wit- 

ched off the electric current to 

Stop the machinery 

This story of amazing fortitude 
was told at the inquest at Lewis- 
ham on Hugh James Pestell, 38, 
of Bourne-way, Hayes, Kent. 

He worked at Woolwich 
Arsenal, where the accident 
eecurred,. 

After switching off the current 
he collapsed and was taken to 
hospital, where he survived for 
seventeen days. 

Dr. Abraham eald at the in- 
quest that no man could have 
been expected to survive more 
than a day after such an accl- 
dent. 

He said Pestell was one in a 
hundred and had marvellous 
vitality and bealth. 
“Accidental death 
verdict. 


was the

from bombed build-

MIRACLE OF 
SURGERY FOR 
BABY'S LIFE 


A wonder operation — 
first in this country—-has 
been performed jin a pid 
to save the life of the 


|newly-born baby of Mrs. 


a ladder, then down five flights. 


Ernest Raymond, wife’ of 


the novelist. 

Mrs. Raymond gave her blood 
to help the surgeons, and the 
operation took place at Middle- 
sex Hospital, London, when the 
baby was only 72 hours old 

Shortly after birth the child 
had been found to be suffer-- 
ing from jaundice. Its condi- 
tion was critical. 

Te one hope lay in removing 
the spleen—a task, it is believed 
never before attempted in this 
country on ome so young. 

A Middlesex Hospital official 
told a reporter:— So far as we 
can discover, this operation has 
never been performed outside the 
United States, where it is rare. 

“When Mrs. Raymond was told 
of the baby’s condition she gave a 
blood transfusion, and later two 
other donours gaye blood.”

MOTIF OF ¥ TORY 


Beethoven did not know 


the Morse code. But he 
wrote in it. By the sheer- 


est and_yet mest -appro-. 


priate tvoincidence pos- 
sible, he employed as a 
rhythmic pattern in the 
first movement of his 
Fifth Symphony what any 
telegrapher would recog- 
nlse-as the Morse symbol 
for the letter V, 

There are two coin- 
cidences here, one of them 
obvious and of no im- 
portance. The letter V is 
the Roman equivalent of 
the figure 5, but what 
matters more is that V 
.has become in France 
and other occupied coun- 


tries a symbol of ultimate: 


victory over the Nazi ter- 
ror. It is written on walls 
and elsewhere to catch 
the eye of Nazi overseers. 
It is ubiquitous. So many 
V’s have appeared as to 
suggest some sort of un- 
derground organisation. 
AS long ago as April this 
newspaper reported on 
this movement. 

Now, like an increasing- 
ly unruly child, V is not 
only going to be seen but 
heard. For victims of 
Nazi ambition are now 
léarning to spell it out in 
dots and dashes; dot-dot- 
dot-dash. They can even 
sing or whistle it, to the 
first four notes of ‘the 
. world’s most ~ familiar 
symphony. If Frenchmen 
do this as often as:senti- 
ment prompts they will 
reproduce most of the 
symphony’s first move- 
ment, which is based on a 
constant reiteration of 
dot-dot-dot-dash motifs, 
one set overlaying another 
in crescendos sometimes 
expressive of an increas- 
ing fury, as of suppressed 
peoples. : 

Nazis seem apprehen- 
sive over this secret wea- 
pon. They are trying to 
cause contusion by adopt- 
ing 'V as a symbol of Nazi 
victory. But those who 
write or sound V ‘for :free- 
dom can, as “Colonel V. 
' Britton” has suggested in 
. a broadcast naming July 

20 .as “V Day,” leave the 
confusion “where it be- 
jongs—in Berlin.” 
.'The Nazis may ‘find 
‘themsélves. in: @ veritable

ta the R.A.F,

IN HIS PRESIDENTIAI 
GRESS IN - EDINBURGH 
PHASISED THE PART TRADE UN 
INTERNATIONAL RELATIONSHIP 
PERMANENT FOUNDATION. 

On the Trade Unionists of this country | 


toes, 


YESTERDAY, WR: 


and on their comrades in-the Dominions and: 
the U.S.A., the task now fell of seeing that 
their.forces and those of their Alfies are pro- 
vided with an overwhelming mechanical and 
fire power in tanks, ‘planes and armoured 
cars and guns of all descriptions. 

With those they should be able to over- 
whelm and finally destroy the malignant 
enemies which menaced everything for which’ 
their movements had _ struggled through 150 
years of effort and sacrifice. 


Referring to the Prime Minis- 
ter, Mr. Gibson said that they 
had had their quarrels with him 
and would have them again but 
at the moment he epitomised the 
tenacious mood to win of — the 
British commonwealth. Never 
was the nation more united. They 
would face any and every hard- 
ship to overthrow Hitlerism. 


Looking to the future. Mr. Gib- 
son said: “Let us, as Trade Un- 
ionists, be worthy of ourselves and 
our history. Let us be sure that 
in the rebuilding of Britain, new: 
cities shali be finer and cleaner 
and great in more than their 
aggregation of population and 
business. 


Just Economics 


“Let us make our economics 
juster and more equitable. Let 
us resolve that our international 
relationships shall be put upon a 
firmer and more permanent 
foundation. In the fulfilment of 
these great, tasks we shall need 
the closest cooperation between 
all our forces, a strong and virile 
trade union movement and a 
powerful and adequately staffed 
labour party.” 


Even in the stress of this titan- 
tic struggle, Mr. Gibson conclud- 
ed, they had preserved much of 
their ancient heritage of freedom. 
They were the only country at 
war in the wotld where bread 
wis not rationed. 

Their Parilament alone 
amongst those of  belfigerents 
had carried out extensive social 
reforms of lasting benefit to 
the tolling masses of this coun- 
try. 


Russia Welcomed 


“Welcoming Russia as an_ ally, 


‘Mr. Gibson’ said’ that this ‘did not 


imply the least sympathy with 
British Communist Party leaders. 
“The astonishing gyrations of 
these people have placed them in 
the lowest category in. the esteem 
of the British “working, class,” 
sald Mr. Gibson. ‘For them. the 
war céased to be an imperialist 
war at 4 a.m. on’ Sunday. June 
22, 1941, when Germany attacked 
Russia and became a righteous 
one.” P ; 
Mr.-Gibson said’ that the “swol- 
len bullfrog of the Pontine Mar- 


_| shes” had got ~what he earned, 
‘| while: Hitler: knew. what 
Tet " 


was 


been a very good one, We are 
striding a little painfully perhaps, 
with joints creaking and groaning 
here and there, but sull striding 
along the high road {ov full indus- 
trial production.” 


Taiking of the wa. aims, Mr. 
Gibson said that, pending the 
creation of a federal Europe, it 
was for the English speaking na- 
tions to show the way.to the 
creation of a working federation 
of nations. 

The United States shold play 
its part with Britain in the pro- 
cess of development, not only on 


_a trade basis, but also in the mat- 


ter of political relations. 
inter-Trading System 


He would, nat debar any con- 
tinental nation: from becoming a 


‘membor of this bloc wiich would 


have for ils main cbject the de- 
velopment of an_ inter-trading 
system for the allotment of raw 
materials, investments, labour 
and skill. 


Referring to American aid, Mr. 
Gibson said: “I have no words 
adequate to express our immense 
gratitude to the people of the 
United S ates and among them, 
especially to the workers of that 
great citadel of liberty, for their 
prompt aii genercus aid to us in 
the hours of our need. 

“Generacians of free men yet 
unborn will bless them for It. 

To our friends across the Atlan- 

tic, | say ‘Weary not in welt- 

doing.’ " . 

One suggestion . made by Mr. 
Gibson in discussing post war con- 
struction was that in collaboration 
with the Dominion Governments, 
the population of the Dominions 
should be increased by the influx 
of those people who, would”. help 
to make them: strong. against: the 
threat. of. further. wars, and: “in 
the process, the ‘industrial de-, 
velopment of the Dominions 
should be aided and-extended by 
a settlement of skilled industrial- 
ists from this country.” .

South’s bidding was optimistic, 
but he was helped by West’s de- 
fence. West opened the King of 
hearts and then continued with 
the heart Queen. That gave 


South the chance to make his 
contract. He noted that setting 
sup his own hand would be difficult 
if not impossible, but that dum- 
my was already set up except for 
the hearts. The slam would be 
made if he could ruff out all of 


‘| dummy’s losing ‘heats; then draw 


trumps from the dummy, and 
run the clubs. 

West had assisted South by 
leading two rounds of hearts, but 
South would still have to get to 
dummy three times more: twice, 
to lead hearts for ruffing, and 
once to draw trumps and run the 
clubs. The three entries were 
€asy to find — two in clubs and 
one in trumps. 


South therefore ruffed the sec- 
ond heart with the spade Queen 


and led the low trump to dummy's ! 


eight. Another heart was ruffed 
by the spade King, and dummy 
was entered via the eight of 
clubs. Dummy’s last heart was 
then ruffed by'the Ace of trumps, 
and dummy was entered for the 
last time with the Jack of clubs. 
Dummy’s trumps were then led 
out, drawing all of East’s trumps 
—and south took care to discard 
the Ace of dlubs so as not to 
biock that ‘suit. From ‘there on, 
dummy had only winning tricks.

“T can Speak only of the Sollum battlefield — 
the coastal plain-and-the-slopes-of-the-escarpment. 
Owing to casualties among ‘the ‘war correspondents’ 
transport, we were unable to follow the battle of 
tanks on the plateau, where enemy dive-bombers 
joined in at a heavy cost in men and machines. 


“The first day’s fighting before 
Sollum ended in our men disput- 
ing possession of the Halfaya 
(Hellfire Pass). At dawn next day, 
undet cover of a heavy ground 
mist, our attack was renewed. 
Both the weather and the terrain 
sulted thé Highlanders, who form- 
ed ‘the left flank. Within two 
hours the enemy was driven out 
of fortified positions at the head 
of the pass. 


“Indian troops on the right 
encountered stronger opposition, 
and never did the pass so well de- 
serve its lurid nickname as it did

“Meanwhile, with the High- 
landers holding the head of the 
pass, another column of our in- 
fantry, supported by a squadron 
of tanks, advanced along the 
ridge of the escarpment, where it 
sweeps in a vast semi-circle to- 
wards the headland behind Sol- 
um. 


“On this headland stands an old 
Egyptian barracks, - gleaming 
white against the brown sand. The 
German garrison there. about a 
battalion strong. abandoned their 
position, and, with the town be- 
low no longer tenable, made their 
way along the shores of Sollum 
Bay to the mouth of Hellfire Pass. 
But there was no escape by the 
pass, The only hope was to with- 
draw ta the beach and make a 
stand wit! their backs to the 
sea. 


Tables Turned 


“There was a ocértain grim, if 
short-lived, satisfaction In the 
knowledge that for once was 
the enemy calied on to defend: 
himselfon a beach. 


“Meanwhile, the Indians in 
Hellfire Pass, fighting tirelessly, 
made good progress, and a few 
Germans who had escaped death 
or capture manned isolated 
machine-guns on the western 
slopes \of the pass. From these 
posts they kept up a harassing fire. 
which took a fairly heavy toll of 
the Indians. 


“The front remained quiet 
throughout the night. From time

Andantino Vartle (In B Minor, H. 


to time the Germans on the beach 
below Sollum sent up Verey lights. 


“The next énemy counter-at- 
tack developed far to the south 
beyond Sidi Omar. 


“A formidable Panzer. (arm- 
oured) force sighted from the 
air was too food a chance for the 
R.A.F. to miss. Nine Blenheims, 
flying in tight formation from a. 
height of 3,000 feet, scattered tanks 
and armoured forces in all direc- 
tions. 


Ten tanks were among the 
wreckage strewn on the desert,” 
but the threat of an encircting 
movement was too serious to be 
ignored, and the order was 
given for a goneral withdrawal 
to our original positions. 


“As our troops pulled back from 
the Hellfire Pass along the ‘Via 
Mussolini’, Panzer squadrons 
loomed over the coastal escarp- 
ment, They remained poised there 
for a moment, and then poured 
down the slopes like dark torrents 
and raced across the coastal plain 
—just too late.” 


BOYS 
CAPTURE 
NAZI 

Three evacuee boys cap- 
tured a wounded Nazi alr- 
man, and took him to a 
police station three miles 
away. = 


The boys, 17-year-old twins, 
Edward and David Smith and 
Desmond Mercer; aged 15, came 
across the airman after they had 
been to see the wreckage of his 
‘plane, one of the bombers shot 
down by our fighters at the night. 


Vv 


“‘We had driven about two miles 
on our homeward journey,” they 
said, “when we saw the airman 
lying beside a lonely road, waving 
his arm feebly.

After spirited bidding, 2,100 guincas 
was paid at Newmarket for a yearling 
Ally by Hyperion from Quashed. 
Hyperion won the Derby for his breed- 
er, Lord Derby, in 1936, the same year 
in which Quashed, by Obliterate, won 
the Ascot Gold Cup over 244m. after a 
great rate .with the American cham- 
pion, Omaha. Quashed was one of the 
greatest sthying mares of her time.

SHIPPING 
LOSSES. 
STILL TOO 
HIGH 


“The Times" publishes a graph, 
analysing British mercantile ship- 
ping losses of the past 30 months, 
and comparing these with losses 
in 1917-18, 

The graph shows that. the 
enemy has not succeeded in this 
wer in reaching the rate of ship- 
ping destruction achieved in April, 
1917, when Britain lost 550,000 
tons. 

Nevertheless, says “The Times,” 
‘he rate of des'ruction in this 
war has been about half of the 
1917 peak level of the past year. 
That level, it says, indicates a 
dangerous situation which was 
not in hand up to June. 

However, a fall in the curve, 
recently, indicates the beginning 
of an improvement. It is to be 
hoped the improvement will be 
maintained, but the public is not 
to know that because regular fig- 
ures will no longer be issued, 


KHSTORIC 
LOCKET 
FOUND 


When, just before laying down 
his head on the executioner's 
block, in 1794, Georges Jacques 
Danton said, “My bed soon will 
be annihilation, but I shall live in 
the Pantheon of history,” little did 
he dream that nearly a century 
and a half later a locket bearing: 
a representation of his head 
would be dug up in a suburban 
garden in far-off Australia. 

The locket was unearthed re- 
cently at Shakespeare Street, Co-| 
orparoo, by Mr. W. L. Nicklin. 
He has presented it to the Mus- 
eum, and the director (Mr.. H. A 
Longman) says it is a valuable ad- 
dition to the institution's collec- 
tion, 

Danton was the leader of the 
movement which resulted in the 
destruction of the Bastille and 
the setting up of the tribunal 
which afterwards included him 
amongst its victims. He, how- 
ever, had said, “I: prefer pene 
guillotined to guillotining, 2 ‘

RUSSIAN 
IN ANZAC 
AIR FORCE 


Vladimir Vitte, 31, 
ed Russian tractor driver, 
Tully, has been accepted for 
crew by Brisbane Air Force 
cruiting authorities. - 

Vitte, who was born and educat- 
ed in China, is believed to be the 
first Russian accepted for air crew 
in Queensland. He has been 16 
years in the State. Recruiting 
officials said that he was a parti- 
cularly fine type of recruit,

SWIMMERS 
HIN TITLES 


A' new American 400 Metres 
tree style relay record was created 
by the Hawaiian swimming team 
on August 24 when they clocked 
4 min. 2.3 sec.—shattering the ex- 
isting record of 4 min. 6.8 sec. 
eetablish¢d by Yale University in' 
1935. 

Participating ino oa Los 
Beles swimming meet, the team 
was composed of Bill Smith, Paul 
Herron, Charles Oda, and Talcashi 
Hirose 

On the same day Chieko Mliya- 
moto placed first in the Women's 
100 Metre back-stroke in 1 min. 
23.4 sec. 

In the 100) Metre free-style 
event on August 25 Hirose won in 
58.7 secs. while Chieko Miyomoto 
clocked 3 mit, 17 sec, to win the 
Women's 200 Metres breast-stroke.

MARQUES - 


(IN SHANGHAI 


BOWLS FINAL 


WINNING CONSISTENTLY ON, 


HALE AND THREE-QUARTER 


ENDS, B. F. MARQUES CREAT- 
ED A MAJOR UPSET IN THE 
SEMI-FINALS_OF THE. LAWN: 
BOWLS SINGLES CHAMPION- 
SHIP, HELD ON AUGUST 26 AT 
THE ‘GREENS OF THE SHANG- 
HAI LAWN BOWLS. CLUB, 
WHEN HE ELIMINATED THE 
STRONGLY FAVOURED A. 
MARSHALL FROM THE CON- 
TEST BY 21—16, WRITES THE 
“NORTH CHINA DAILY NEWS.” 

It was a touch-and-go proposi- 
tion all the way through and even 
until the 24th end the outcome 
still hung in the balance as, had 
any one of Marshal]'s shots car- 
the score would 
have been tied and the game ex- 
tended to 25 ends. 

Meanwhile in the second semi- 
final tussle, W. R. Chisholm justi- 
fled his early favouritism to 
triumph convincingly over his 
Club mate, A. B. Hogg. by the 
score of 21--14, The encounter 
was productive of a much higher 
brand of bowls than that in’ the 
Marques- Marshall fixture as 
woods by both players were laid 
close to the jack.

Lieut. T. A. Pearce, Colony 
and Hong Kong Cricket Club 
all-rounder, will be in charge of 
‘the First Division team, and it is 
felt that with one of his calibre 
at the helm the season’s prospects 
for Army are indeed- bright.’ 

Though no names are available just: 
yot, it Is understood that tn: addition to 
most of last seagonjs stalwarts, Pearce |' 
will also have the services of a. couple 
of new arrivals. who. hava Played in 
gaod class cricket at Home and. who 
are expected to be able to. turn aut 
regularly 


In addition to Alec Rearca, two Bong 
Kong Cricket Club players in J. L, C, 
Pearce and R, M. M, King are also 
avaliable, 

Major Swyer, who. has captained 
R.A.M.C. with so much success for a 
‘number of years, will be. leading, the 
Second Division team, and will ‘have 
‘some promising talent from which to 
make his selection, 


By. Drawhack 


The one great drawback for the 
Army lis the lack of facilities for nets 
practice—at least Insofar as ‘‘other 
ranks" are concerned—and in this con- 
nection tt would perhaps be worth the 
while of the Sports Board to consider 
returfing part of the ground at Soakun- 
poo for this purpose, 

Owing to the fact that the different 
unith are scattered all over the Colony, 
organised practice is almost an impos- 
sibility for the Army, and. knock-ups on 
cement pltches, in barrack compounds, 
have served for practices in the past— 
aad will probably continue to do so for 
many years to come 

In spite of all this, however, Army 
have always been able to provide the 
Colony with a number of really good 
all-round cricketers, and It would ap- 
pear that 1941-42 will be no exception. 

Now that the Army have — openly 
stated they are ready for the season, 
it is to be hoped that the Cricket 
League will rise from tts prolonged 
Summer slumber and set the wheels 
in motion for another enjoyable season.

A MASS REPRESENTATIVE meeting was held 
at the King’s Theatre at noon to-day to bid farewell 


and to make presentations on beh: 


of the com- 


munity to His Excellency the Governor, Sir-Geofiry 


é ‘Narthcote, and Lady Northcote, on the eve of ‘their 
departure. 


Heads of Departments and the Services, mem- 
bers of the Executive, Legislative and Urban Coun- 
cils, members of the Consular Body, and representa- 
tives of numerous organisations in the Colony at- 


tended the meeting, while 


the Gallery was packed 


with members of the general public. 


His Excellency was presented 
with a large folding screen bear- 
ing the valedictory address made ; 
by the Hon. Sir Robert Kotewall, 
Kt. C.M.G., LL.D., on behalf ol 
the Chinese community. 

At the conclusion of His Excel- 
lency’s farewell speech, Lady 
Northcote was presented with a 
bouquet by Miss Rita Lo, daugh- 
ter of the Hon, Mr. M. K. and 
Mrs. Lo. ; 

The Valedictory Address read 
on behalf of the community of 
Hong Kong by the Hon. Mr, J. J. 
Paterson was as follows: 

The members of this commun- 
ity regret that ill health should 
necessitate your departure from 
Hong Kong. 

Your Excelency has done much 
for Hong Kong during your term 
of office here and those of us whu 
have had the honour of working 
for you know how important the 
common weal was in your eyes. 
We recognise your great interest 
in social services, one of the sor- 
rows of war is that humanity is 
set back, things ‘essential in peace 
time must wait Until the war effort 
is fully served and victory won. 
But we do thank you for the very 
great deal that you have done for 
us. 

And so good-bye. May this com- 
munity wish Your Excellency re- 
newed health in a more kindly 
climate and to yuu and Ladv 
Northcote the very best of good 
fortune, 


Chinese Address

read the Valedictory Address on 
behalf of the Chinese Community, 
of which the following is a 
translation: 

In the ‘Book of Changes’ it is 
said: “If the right man be not in 
office, right’ principles of govern- 
ment will not prevail.” By pro- 
moting international friendship 
and by paying regard to public 
opinion, Tzu Ch’an’ governed the 
State of Cheng; by practising: 
‘sincerity and by dispensing jus- 
tice, K’ung Ming governed: the-

” MOTIF.OF VICTORY 


Beethoven did not:know 
the Morse code. _ But he 
"wréte in it: By the sheer- 
est-and yet most appro- 
priate coincidence pos- 
sible, he employed as a 
rhythmic pattern in the 
first movement of his 
Fifth Symphony what any 
telegrapher would recog- 
nisé as the Morse symbol 
for'the letter V. 


There are two coin- 
cidences here, one of them 
obvious and of no im- 
portance. The letter V is 
the Roman equivalent of 
the figure 5, but what 
matters more is that V 
has become in France| 
and other occupied coun- 
tries a symbol of ultimate 
victory over the Nazi ter- 
ror. It is written on -walts 
and elsewhere to catch 
the eye of Nazi overseers. 
It is ubiquitous. So ‘many 
V’s have appeared as to 
Suggest some sort Of un- 
ferground organisation. 
j As long ago as April this 

newspaper reported on 
this movement. 

Now, like an increasing- 
ly unruly child, Vis not 
only going to be seen but 
heard. For victims of 
Nazi ambition are now 
learning to spell it out in 
dots and dashes; dot-dot- 
dot-dash. They can even 
sing or whistle it, to the 
first four notes of _the

THE SHANGHAI TWO-WEEK] 
LONG “BATTLE OF BEAUTIES” 


IN THE BRITISH COURT END- 
ED THIS MORNING WITH THE 
REGISTRAR, MR, G. H. HAINES, 
GIVING JUDGMENT TO. THE 
EFFECT THAT BOTH baaTies 
WERE EQUALLY TO BLAME. 
¢ The assault action was original- 
ly brought” by “Wiss Mercia - 
Scharnhorst against Miss Elsie 
Cruz, but subsequent hearings 
revealed that a fight had ensued 
between defendant pee fone mem- 
bers: of plaintiff’s family. over’ a 
bathroom dispute. ke Se ge 
Miss Cruz bore visible evid- 
ence duting the procecdings, 
nursing a black eye and bites 
on the arm. x 
The Registrar condemned the 
case as “disgraceful for British 
subjects." Miss Cruz later filed 
counter-charges and was award- 
ed $50 dumages for medical costs, 
while Mtss Scharnhorst was 
bound over on recognizances in 
the sum of $250 and two sure- 
ties of $250 each.—International 
News Service.

“SNATCHING GANG 


A gang of alleged wrist-watch 
snatchers, was charged before 
Mr. H. G. Sheldon, K.C., at the 
Central Magistracy this morning. 
. Tong Lai-hung, 17, was charg- 
ed wih = snatching wrist-watches 
from Miss Josephine Chow, of 
No, 49. Village Road, ground 
floor; and from Miss Ng Siu-tin 
6 Kat Tak Road. A 23- 
year-old married woman, Cheung 
Sen-fong, was charged with re- 
ceiving stolen property. 


Lo Pan, 18, 
were charged 


and Tse Tok, 21, 
with stealing a 
Wrist-watch from a = /Chinese 
woman in Fenwick Street yes- 
terday, and Kwok Choi, 24, mar- 
ried woman, was charged with 
receiving. 

Several wrist-watches were 
found in accused's possession. 
The case was adjourned for 72 
hours for further enquiries.

brought for trial by Judge Neu-|

ON A FIRMER AND MORE’. 


On the Trade Unionists of this’ country 
and on their comrades in the Dominions and 
the U.S.A., the task now fell of seeing that 
their forces and those of their Allies are pro- 
vided with an overwhelming mechanical and 
fire power in tanks, ‘planes and armoured 
cars and guns of all descriptions. 

‘ With those they should be able to over- 
whelm and finally destroy the malignant 
enemies which menaced everything for which 
their movements had struggled through 150 
years of effort and sacrifice. 


Referring to the Prime Minis- 
ter, Mr. Gibson said that they 
had had their quarrels with him 
and would have them again but 
at the moment he epitomised the 
tenaclous mood to win of the 
British commonwealth. Neyer 
was the nation more united. They 
would face any and every hard- 
ship to overthrow Hitlerism. 


Looking to the future, Mr. Gib- 
son said: “Let us, as Trade Un. 
ionists, be worthy of ourselves and 
our history. Let us be sure that 
in the rebuilding of Britain, new 
cities shall be finer and cleaner

A HIGHLY BELLICOSE SPEECH on the 
alleged encirclement of Japan by the ABCD 
front was broadcast throughout Japan yester- 
. day by Colonel Hayao Mabuchi, in an “East 
Asia Day” programme. 


Colonel Mabuchi is the Japanese Army 


press chief and his ‘death struggle” 


broad- 


cast is sharply contrasted with the cautious 
tones of the military leaders in Tokyo. 


Leaflets 
Dropped 


In Iran 


“All Germans in Iran 
are spies and enemies of 
the Iranian people,” say 
leaflets dropped over 
Teheran by a Soviet ‘plane 
yesterday. 

“They want to muke slaves of 
the [ranfans.” 

The leaflets warm 
that the Germans, for their own 
interests, desire to draw Iran 
into a war against thelr friends, 
Russia and Britain. 

“People of Ivan, the hour has 
atrived for you to end Hitler’s 
intrigues in Iran. Britain and 
Russia have taken steps to this 
end. 

“Death to  Hitlerismm, which 
wgints to make Iran like other 
counties the Nazis have con. 
quered. 

“Long Live 
Friendship.” 

Leaflets on these lines were 
dropped both yesterday and Sun- 
day. — Reuter.

Very often. the correct play of 


a hand ts to raff -your losers in|; 


the dummy and: set up your. own; 
but occasionally the protess;must 
be reversed. Then you must ruil, 
dummy’s losers in your own. hahidy’ 


treating the dummy as the’ master 


hand and your own hand ‘as! the 
ordinary sort of dummy. ‘For 
example:

South's bidding was optimistic, 
but he was helped by West's de- 
fence. West opened the King of 
hearts and then continued with 
the heart Queen. That gave 


South the chance to make his 
contract. He noted that setting 
up his own hand would be difficult 


if not impossible, but that dum- 
my was already set up except for 
the heartse The slam would be 
made if he could ruff out all of 
dummy’s losing hearts, then draw 
trumps from the dummy, and 
run the clubs. 


West had assisted South by 
leading two rounds of hearts, but 
South would stili have fo get to 
dummy three times more: twice 
to lead hearts for ruffing, and 
once to draw trumps and run the 
clubs, The three entries were! 
easy to find — two in clubs and: 
one in trumps, 


South therefore ruffed the sec- 
ond heart with the spade Queen 
and led the low trump to dummy’s 
eight. Another heart was ruffed 
by the spade King, and dunimy 
was entered via the eight of 
clubs. Dummy’'s last heart was 
‘then ruffed by the Ace of trumps, 
and dummy was entered for the 
last time with the Jack of clubs. 
Dummy’s trumps were theh led 
out, drawing all of East’s trumps’ 
—and south took care to discard 
the Ace of clubs so as not- to 
‘Block that suit, From there’ on, 
‘dummy had only winning tricks,

THE CHINA MAIL, 


INSPIRING = 


FAREWELL 
SPEECH BY 
THE GOVERNO 


(Continued from Page 6). 
on the one hand the deepest re- 
gret that I must give up my work 
with but little done of what I had 
hoped to do, and ata time of 
<erisis for the Colony and the-Em- 
pire; on the other, warm gratitude 
for the help that I have had 
throughout my term of office both 
from official and unofficial - col- 
leagues, and heartfelt appreciation 
of what has been suid this morn- 
ing of my endeavours and of what 
stands Inscribed on this beautiful 


, screen, 


The Contrast 


Recently Ihave read over the 
speeches which Sir Andrew Cal- 
decott made at the time of his bid- 
ding the Colony farewell and I 
was most forcibly struck by the 
contrast of the circumstances as 
they were then and as they are 
to-day. When Sir Andrew wis 
speaking not only the British’ Em- 
pire but China herself was still at 
peace, and throughout those two 
speeches there is no word or hint 
of war; to us to-day such an 
atmosphere is almost unthinkable, 
IT can only hope that a good part 
of Sir Mark Young's term of office 
will be free from that grievous 
blight. 

To turn for u moment to the 
eulogistic addresses which have 
been offered to me this morning, 
T can only say that I am modestly, 
not to say painfully, aware of the 
great discrepancy between the 
person described therein and the 
reality who stands here acknow- 
ledge them. Indeed I am reminded 
of a story told of Lord Justice 
Bowen, when a committee of 
Judges was drafting an address to 
Queen Victoria. A member oru- 
posed that the address should be- 
gin with the words ‘Conscious as 
we are of our shortcomings’ to 
which Lord Bowen — rejoined 
‘Would it not be truer to say’: 
“Conscious as we are of each 
other's shortcomings.”’ It is of 
course the exact converse of that 
story which applies in this case, 
for 1 feel most truly conscious of 
how far short I fall of the virtues 
ascribed to me in these addresses. 
But for all that, my heart is tru- 
ly glowing with thankfulness and 
pride that I should be spoken of 
in those terms; and [ cannot ade- 
quately express my gratitude to 
their authors. 


Social Welfare 


1 will not burden you with a 
recitation of all my many dreams 
of what might be done here dur- 
ing my Governorship, but I will,

if you ‘will allow me, mention two 
+ Which are so near to my heart that 
I feel that I must commend them 
‘to your keeping. We are rightly 
proud in Hong Kong of our great 
charitable institutions, and I most 
fully agree with all that Sir An- 
drew Caldecott said on that sub- 
ject. At the-same time I have 
felt for a Jong time that there {s a 
need for organised research {nto 
and scientific study of the whole 
field of social welfare in this 
Colony, and before I went on leave 
last year I was planning the ap- 
pointment of a Standing Commis- 
sion for Social Welfare Affairs, the 
object of which would have been 
to carry on a continuous survey of - 
and research into social conditions 
in this Colony with a view to their 
steady betterment. I visualised 
that Commission not merely as 
a research body. however: It was 
to be Government's consultant on 
such matters and, should occasion 
so require, Government's stimula- 
tor and critic. Its duties 
would have been to sug- 
gest for Government's , con- 
sideration and execution polic- 
tes and programmes of a long- 
ange order in connection with 
public health, town planning, 
housing, labour conditions, educa- 
tion, and so forth. An explora- 
tory Committee was appointed but 
before it could get to work there 
came the collapse of France and 
the immediate need for intensive 
development of the whole of our 
civil defence organisation. This in 
one way or another has so. ab- 
sorbed attention that no progress 
with the proposal to set up. this 
Commission was possible: and. 
though since my return I have 
alven the question close considera- 
tion, I fear that no other conclu- 


sion can be rightly reached at the 
present time. 


A Reproach 


Nevertheless I trust that some 
day that Standing Commission will 
come into being. Governors come 
and Governors go and with each 
change there is an interregnum 
and then the period of ‘picking 
up the strings’: on the other hand, 
it is of essential importance that 
the major problems of social wel- 
fare shou'd be under continuous 
surveillance and social ills under 
continuous treatment, irrespective 
of the occupancy of Government 
House at any one time. Thai 
Hong Kong is not as up-to-date as 
we would wish in the social wel- 
fare activities which I have named 
cannot be denied. A Standing 
Commission ensuring continuity of 
study and remedial action would, 
I am _ confident, do much in time 
to relieve us of that. reproach. 

The other dream which I be- 
queath to you Wwas' the recovery of 


f. the immensely valuable lahds in 


the hearts. of our towns, to-day, 
occupied by s8idiers 
gladiy go to healthier and more 
roomy surroundings, 

brake out in 1939 I had obtaine 
the sanction of the War Office and 
the Colonial Office to place before 
the Legislature a scheme to re-

In the Hong Kong All-Stars v U. S. Navy 
series, the blue-jackets recovered from their 
shellacking of a week ago and managed to pull 
the series out of the fire with a 7 to 3 triumph 
after the count had been dead-locked 3-all for 


six eventful frames. 


Hong Kong Baseballers |high-stepper rifled a bullet peg 


v. South China the 
League finale, resulted 
in Mohawks over-running 
Caroliners 18 to 6 before 
a handful of spectators, 
whilst the Bees shaded the 
Aces 4 to 2 in an exhibi- 
tion tilt staged by the 
Pupils of Grandpa Leung's 
Baseball School. 


Alt Lau, who started on the 
hill for the Chinese, was derrick - 
ed in favour of Wally China after 
he had) conceded three safeties, 
For the rest of the game Ching 
fanned three and walked one, 
and was rebbed of a nou-hit no- 
run performance by allowing Lou 
Pawloski_ to nick him for a 
double. Tony Mascavage, work- 
Ing on the slab for the losers, 
yielded seven blows, struek-out 
one and walked three. 


Short-lived Lead 
Three Chinese batters paraded 
lo the plate and back 
again in the first inning for a

blows to jump into a short-lived

whilst) Hank Chan) was given a 
life on third-sacker Bowersox’s 
fumble. Al Lau trotted down the

ing moundsinan Mascavtage for a 
walk on four pitched balls, 
Coming up into the batter's

bace-tralile, Pete Choy socked tho 
apple on the nose for a two-run 
double. Dick Chung's “Japanese 
liner’ to right chased two moro 
matea over (the plate for the 
Chinese to draw ahead, and be- 
fore the disastrous Inning was 
over Hank All dumped a neat 
bunt along first to score Chung. 
In the next semester the Chin- 
ese started off by loading the sacks 
on a bingle, a miscue and a walk 
to produce another cluster of five 
counters, after they had batted 
. Tight around. In the fourth ,and 
the fifth they were horse-collared, 
but in the sixth the Chinese 
stowed the game away with an- 
other marker on a walk and a 
Sacrifice.

“AFTER MORE THAN a year of oppres- 
sion, the Netherlands are and feel stronger, 
and are more unconguerable than ever be- 
for,” declared Her Majesty Queen Withel- 
mina over Radio Orange last night, broad- 
casting to Dutch people throughout the world 
im commemoration - of, her 61st birthday 


which was celebrated on Sunday. 

She declared that the Netherlands would 
play its part in the new world order as en- 
visaged by Mr. Winston Churchill and Pre- 


sident Roosevelt. 

The arcn-enemy of, humanity, 
Hitier, wanted to destroy the 
Netherlands. He had over- 

wered them, robbed them of 
}iberty and his hordes had looted, 
Jeaving the Dutch to starve. He 
sovght to crush their soul but had 
failed. 

She praised the “indomitable 
courage, daring and tough un- 
preakable resistance” of th 


now venting his lust for murder 
and robbery against the courage- 
ous Russian people, resistance 
manifests itself as his back is 
turned and the united peoples are 
preparing at present to toll the 
death-knell for him and his 
detestable order.

Admiral 
Resign 

Admiral Leluc has re- 
signed command of the 
French Naval Forces and 
his post as head of the 
Navy Secretariat which 
was entrusted to him by 
Admiral Darlan, says the} 
Vichy News Agency. 

He is replaced in both posts 
by Admiral Auphan, who is the! 
youngest officer holding ,an Ad- 
miral’s rank in the French Navy. 

Admiral Auphan was entrusted 
with the reorganisation of the 
French mercantile marine. 

Admiral Leluc was one of the 
French _ plenipotentiaries who 
signed the Armistice with Ger- 
many. He--was regarded in 
French naval circles as a very 
competent sailor for whom a 
brillant career. was predicted. — 


Reuter.

The No. 1 Typhoon Signal 
was hoisted in the Colony 
at 2.10 p.m. to-day. 

The Royal Observatory re- 
ceived reports this morning 
that the position of the 
typhoon at 6 a.m.. to-day 
was Lat. 17, Long. 115, 
moving in a westerly direc- 
tion. 

Local observations,” how- 


ever, indicate that the -- 


typhoon has recurved and 
moving in a north-westerly 
direction.
